To find the limit of e^x divided by x squared as x approaches infinity, I start by analyzing the behavior of both functions individually. 

First, I recognize that e^x grows exponentially, while x^2 grows polynomially. Exponential functions generally grow much faster than polynomial functions as x becomes very large.

Next, I consider using L'Hospital's Rule since both the numerator and the denominator approach infinity as x approaches infinity, which means the limit is in an indeterminate form of ∞/∞.

Applying L'Hospital's Rule involves taking the derivatives of the numerator and the denominator. The derivative of e^x is still e^x, and the derivative of x^2 is 2x. This gives us a new fraction: (e^x) / (2x).

I check if this new limit also falls into an indeterminate form as x approaches infinity. Here, e^x again grows much faster than 2x, so we apply L'Hospital's Rule once more.

Taking the derivative of the numerator e^x gives e^x, and the derivative of 2x is 2. This results in (e^x) / 2.

Finally, as x approaches infinity, e^x grows without bound while the denominator remains a constant 2. Therefore, the limit approaches infinity.
</think>

To find the limit:

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{e^{x}}{x^{2}}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Behavior of Functions:**
   - As \( x \) approaches infinity, both \( e^{x} \) and \( x^{2} \) increase without bound.
   - However, exponential functions grow much faster than polynomial functions.

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   - Since both the numerator and denominator approach infinity, we can apply L'Hospital's Rule:
     \[
     \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{e^{x}}{x^{2}} = \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{\frac{d}{dx} e^{x}}{\frac{d}{dx} x^{2}} = \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{e^{x}}{2x}
     \]
   - The new limit is still in the indeterminate form \( \frac{\infty}{\infty} \), so we apply L'Hospital's Rule again.

3. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule a Second Time:**
     \[
     \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{e^{x}}{2x} = \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{\frac{d}{dx} e^{x}}{\frac{d}{dx} 2x} = \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{e^{x}}{2}
     \]

4. **Evaluate the Final Limit:**
   - As \( x \) approaches infinity, \( e^{x} \) grows without bound.
   - Therefore:
     \[
     \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{e^{x}}{2} = \infty
     \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\infty}
\]

\frac{1 - \cos(0)}{0^2} = \frac{0}{0}
\]

1. Differentiate the numerator \(1 - \cos x\) to get \(\sin x\).
2. Differentiate the denominator \(x^2\) to get \(2x\).

Applying L'Hôpital's Rule once, we have:

\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin x}{2x}

Substituting \(x = 0\) gives another indeterminate form \(\frac{0}{0}\). Apply L'Hôpital's Rule again.

1. Differentiate the numerator \(\sin x\) to get \(\cos x\).
2. Differentiate the denominator \(2x\) to get \(2\).

\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\cos x}{2}

2 - 2 = 0

So, substituting gives us 0/0, which is an indeterminate form. That means we can't determine the limit just by plugging in x = 2 directly. We need another approach.

Now, I recall that when you end up with a 0/0 form after substitution, it usually means there's some simplification possible. Specifically, factoring both the numerator and denominator to see if any common terms can be canceled out might help.

Let's factor the numerator (x² - 4). I know that this is a difference of squares because x² is a square and 4 is also a square (2²).

So, we can write:

x² - 4 = (x + 2)(x - 2)

That factors neatly into two binomials: (x + 2) and (x - 2). 

Now, looking at the denominator, which is just (x - 2), I notice that both the numerator and denominator share a common factor of (x - 2). This means we can cancel out these terms.

So, simplifying the expression:

(x² - 4)/(x - 2) = [(x + 2)(x - 2)] / (x - 2)

After canceling (x - 2):

= x + 2

lim_{x → 2} (x + 2) = 2 + 2 = 4

Therefore, after simplifying and canceling the common terms, the limit of the original function as x approaches 2 is 4.

The given problem involves finding the limit of a rational function:

\[
\lim_{x \to 2} \frac{x^2 - 4}{x - 2}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Substitution:** 
   First, attempt to substitute \( x = 2 \) directly into the function:
   \[
   \frac{2^2 - 4}{2 - 2} = \frac{0}{0}
   \]
   This results in an indeterminate form (\( \frac{0}{0} \)), indicating that direct substitution does not determine the limit.

2. **Factorization:**
   Factor both the numerator and denominator:
   \[
   x^2 - 4 = (x + 2)(x - 2)
   \]
   The expression becomes:
   \[
   \frac{(x + 2)(x - 2)}{x - 2}
   \]

3. **Simplification:**
   Cancel the common \( (x - 2) \) factor in both the numerator and denominator, provided \( x \neq 2 \):
   \[
   x + 2
   \]

4. **Evaluate the Limit:**
   Now, evaluate the simplified expression as \( x \) approaches 2:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 2} (x + 2) = 2 + 2 = 4
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The limit is \(\boxed{4}\).

The limit of \( f(x) = x^x \) as \( x \) approaches \( 0^+ \) can be found by taking the natural logarithm, simplifying, and applying L’Hospital’s Rule.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Express the function with logarithms:**
   Let \( f(x) = x^x \).
   Taking the natural logarithm:
   \[
   \ln(f(x)) = \ln(x^x) = x \cdot \ln(x)
   \]

2. **Find the limit of the transformed expression:**
   We need to compute:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0^+} x \cdot \ln(x)
   \]
   
3. **Rewrite as a fraction for L’Hospital’s Rule:**
   \[
   x \cdot \ln(x) = \frac{\ln(x)}{1/x}
   \]
   As \( x \to 0^+ \), this becomes an indeterminate form \( (-\infty)/\infty \).

4. **Apply L’Hospital’s Rule:**
   Differentiate numerator and denominator:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} \ln(x) = \frac{1}{x}, \quad \frac{d}{dx} \left( \frac{1}{x} \right) = -\frac{1}{x^2}
   \]
   So,
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{\ln(x)}{1/x} = \lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{1/x}{-1/x^2} = \lim_{x \to 0^+} -x = 0
   \]

5. **Exponentiate the result:**
   Since \( \ln(f(x)) \) approaches 0,
   \[
   f(x) = e^{0} = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The limit is 1.

- **Denominator**: The highest power of \( x \) is also 2.

### Step 2: Determine the Leading Coefficients
- **Leading coefficient of the numerator**: 1 (from \( x^2 \))
- **Leading coefficient of the denominator**: 1 (from \( x^2 \))

### Step 3: Calculate the Limit
When both polynomials have the same degree, the limit is simply the ratio of their leading coefficients.

***********************<think>
To find the limit of ( (1 + x³)^(1/3) - 1 ) / (x - 1) as x approaches 1, I can use L'Hospital's Rule because directly substituting x = 1 results in a 0/0 indeterminate form.

First, I differentiate both the numerator and the denominator with respect to x. 

The derivative of the denominator, which is simply d/dx (x - 1), is 1.

For the numerator, using the chain rule: 
d/dx [(1 + x³)^(1/3)] = (1/3)(1 + x³)^(-2/3) * 3x² = x² / (1 + x³)^(2/3).

Now, applying L'Hospital's Rule:
lim x→1 [ ( (1 + x³)^(1/3) - 1 )' ] / (x - 1)' = lim x→1 [x² / (1 + x³)^(2/3)] / 1.

Substituting x = 1 into the simplified expression gives:
(1²) / (1 + 1³)^(2/3) = 1 / (2)^(2/3).

This can be rewritten as 1/(2^(2/3)) or 2^(-2/3).
</think>

To evaluate the limit:

\[
\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(1 + x^3)^{1/3} - 1}{x - 1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Direct Substitution:**  
   First, try substituting \( x = 1 \) into the expression:
   \[
   \frac{(1 + 1^3)^{1/3} - 1}{1 - 1} = \frac{2^{1/3} - 1}{0}
   \]
   This results in an indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \).

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**  
   Since we have an indeterminate form, we can apply L'Hospital's Rule, which states that if \( \lim_{x \to c} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \frac{0}{0} \) or \( \frac{\infty}{\infty} \), then:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to c} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{x \to c} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}
   \]
   
   Differentiate the numerator and the denominator with respect to \( x \):
   
   - **Numerator:**  
     Let \( f(x) = (1 + x^3)^{1/3} - 1 \).
     Using the chain rule:
     \[
     f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(1 + x^3)^{1/3} = \frac{1}{3}(1 + x^3)^{-2/3} \cdot 3x^2 = \frac{x^2}{(1 + x^3)^{2/3}}
     \]
   
   - **Denominator:**  
     Let \( g(x) = x - 1 \).
     \[
     g'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x - 1) = 1
     \]
   
   Now, apply L'Hospital's Rule:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{\frac{x^2}{(1 + x^3)^{2/3}}}{1} = \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{x^2}{(1 + x^3)^{2/3}}
   \]

3. **Evaluate the Limit:**  
   Substitute \( x = 1 \) into the simplified expression:
   \[
   \frac{1^2}{(1 + 1^3)^{2/3}} = \frac{1}{2^{2/3}}
   \]
   
4. **Simplify (Optional):**  
   The expression \( 2^{-2/3} \) can also be written as:
   \[
   2^{-2/3} = \frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{2^2}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{4}}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

```json
{'Final Answer': 'The limit is equal to the cube root of one fourth, which can be represented as 2 raised to the power of -2/3'}
```

To find the derivative of the function:

\[
f(x) = x^3 + 2x^2 + x + 1
\]

we'll differentiate each term separately using basic differentiation rules.

### Step 1: Differentiate Each Term

1. **Differentiate \( x^3 \):**
   - Using the power rule, \(\frac{d}{dx} x^n = n x^{n-1}\).
   - Here, \( n = 3 \), so:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} x^3 = 3x^2
     \]

2. **Differentiate \( 2x^2 \):**
   - Apply the power rule.
   - Here, \( n = 2 \), so:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} 2x^2 = 2 \times 2x = 4x
     \]

3. **Differentiate \( x \):**
   - Using the power rule for \( n = 1 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} x = 1
     \]

4. **Differentiate the constant term \( 1 \):**
   - The derivative of a constant is zero.
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} 1 = 0
     \]

### Step 2: Combine All Derivatives

Adding up all the derivatives:
\[
f'(x) = 3x^2 + 4x + 1 + 0 = 3x^2 + 4x + 1
\]

### Final Answer

The derivative of \( f(x) = x^3 + 2x^2 + x + 1 \) is:

\[
\boxed{3x^2 + 4x + 1}
\]

The quotient rule states that if \( f(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), then:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

- Then, \( v(x) = x^2 + 1 \)

- \( u'(x) = 0 \) (since the derivative of a constant is zero)
- \( v'(x) = 2x \) 

Applying the quotient rule:

v(x) = x^2

To differentiate this product, I should use the product rule. The product rule states that the derivative of u*v is u'v + uv'.

Let me compute each part separately:
- First, find u'(x): The derivative of 2x with respect to x is 2.
- Next, find v'(x): Since v(x) = e^(x²), we'll apply the chain rule again. The derivative is e^(x²) multiplied by the derivative of x², which is 2x. So v'(x) = e^(x²) * 2x.

First term: 2 * e^(x²) remains as is. Second term: 2x multiplied by 2x gives 4x², and that's all multiplied by e^(x²). So the second term becomes 4x² * e^(x²).

The second derivative of F(x) = e^{x²} is calculated as follows:

1. **First Derivative**: 
   Using the chain rule:
   \[
   F'(x) = 2x \cdot e^{x^2}
   \]

2. **Second Derivative**:
   Applying the product rule to F'(x):
   \[
   F''(x) = (2x)' \cdot e^{x^2} + 2x \cdot (e^{x^2})'
   \]
   Calculate each part:
   - \( (2x)' = 2 \)

   \[
   F''(x) = 2 \cdot e^{x^2} + 2x \cdot 2x \cdot e^{x^2}
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   F''(x) = (2 + 4x^2) \cdot e^{x^2}
   \]

The limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{1 - \cos x}{x^2} \) can be found using either a Taylor series expansion or L'Hospital's Rule.

**Using Taylor Series:**
\[
\cos x = 1 - \frac{x^2}{2!} + \frac{x^4}{4!} - \cdots
\]
Subtracting this from 1:
\[
1 - \cos x = \frac{x^2}{2} - \frac{x^4}{24} + \cdots
\]
Dividing by \( x^2 \):
\[
\frac{1 - \cos x}{x^2} = \frac{\frac{x^2}{2}}{x^2} - \frac{\frac{x^4}{24}}{x^2} + \cdots = \frac{1}{2} - \frac{x^2}{24} + \cdots
\]
As \( x \) approaches 0, the higher-order terms vanish, leaving:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{1 - \cos x}{x^2} = \frac{1}{2}
\]

**Using L'Hospital's Rule:**
Since directly substituting \( x = 0 \) gives an indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \), apply L'Hospital's Rule:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{1 - \cos x}{x^2} = \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sin x}{2x}
\]
This still results in \( \frac{0}{0} \), so apply L'Hospital's Rule again:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\cos x}{2} = \frac{\cos 0}{2} = \frac{1}{2}
\]

**Final Answer**

To find the limit of √(x² + 2x) minus x as x approaches infinity, I'll start by simplifying the expression.

First, factor out x squared from inside the square root:
√(x² (1 + 2/x)) = |x| times sqrt(1 + 2/x). Since we're taking the limit as x approaches positive infinity, |x| is just x. So it becomes x times sqrt(1 + 2/x).

Next, expand sqrt(1 + 2/x) using a binomial approximation for large x:
sqrt(1 + ε) ≈ 1 + (ε/2) - (ε²)/8, where ε = 2/x.

Substituting back in:
x times [1 + (2/(2x)) - ((2/x)^2)/8] = x times [1 + 1/x - (4)/(8x²)] = x + 1 - (1)/(2x).

Finally, subtracting x from the expression gives us the limit as x approaches infinity:
(x + 1 - 0) - x = 1.
</think>

To evaluate the limit:

\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Factor Out \( x^2 \):**
   
   Start by rewriting the expression inside the square root.
   
   \[
   \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} = \sqrt{x^2 \left(1 + \frac{2}{x}\right)} = |x| \cdot \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}}
   \]
   
   Since \( x \) approaches infinity, we can consider \( |x| = x \).
   
   So,
   
   \[
   \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} = x \cdot \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}}
   \]

2. **Expand the Square Root:**
   
   Use a binomial approximation for large \( x \):
   
   \[
   \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} \approx 1 + \frac{1}{x} - \frac{(2/x)^2}{8} = 1 + \frac{1}{x} - \frac{1}{2x^2}
   \]
   
3. **Multiply by \( x \):**
   
   Multiply the approximation back by \( x \):
   
   \[
   x \cdot \left(1 + \frac{1}{x} - \frac{1}{2x^2}\right) = x + 1 - \frac{1}{2x}
   \]
   
4. **Subtract \( x \):**
   
   Now, subtract \( x \) from the expression:
   
   \[
   \left(x + 1 - \frac{1}{2x}\right) - x = 1 - \frac{1}{2x}
   \]
   
5. **Take the Limit as \( x \to \infty \):**
   
   As \( x \) approaches infinity, the term \( \frac{1}{2x} \) approaches zero.
   
   Therefore,
   
   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \left(1 - \frac{1}{2x}\right) = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{1}

The limit of \( x^x \) as \( x \) approaches \( 0^+ \) is calculated by recognizing that this expression can be rewritten using natural logarithms and evaluated to approach 1.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Expression Setup:** We start with the limit \( \lim_{x \to 0^+} x^x \).

2. **Natural Logarithm Transformation:** Let \( y = x^x \). Taking the natural logarithm of both sides gives:
   \[
   \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x)
   \]

3. **Evaluate the Limit of ln(y):** Compute \( \lim_{x \to 0^+} x \cdot \ln(x) \).

4. **Indeterminate Form:** As \( x \to 0^+ \), \( \ln(x) \to -\infty \). This results in an indeterminate form of \( 0 \times (-\infty) \).

5. **Rewrite for L'Hospital's Rule:** Express the product as a fraction:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{\ln(x)}{1/x}
   \]
   This is now in the form \( -\infty/\infty \), suitable for applying L'Hospital's Rule.

6. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   - Derivative of numerator (\( \ln(x) \)) is \( 1/x \).
   - Derivative of denominator (\( 1/x \)) is \( -1/x^2 \).

7. **Simplify After Differentiation:**
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{1/x}{-1/x^2} = \lim_{x \to 0^+} (-x) = 0
   \]

8. **Exponentiate to Find y:** Since \( \ln(y) \to 0 \), we have:
   \[
   y = e^0 = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The limit of \( x^x \) as \( x \) approaches \( 0^+ \) is 1.

The limit as \( x \) approaches \( 0^+ \) of \( \frac{\ln(1 + x)}{x} \) can be found using L'Hospital's Rule or recognizing the Taylor series expansion. Both methods lead to the conclusion that the limit is 1.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Form:** As \( x \) approaches 0, both numerator \( \ln(1 + x) \) and denominator \( x \) approach 0, resulting in a \( \frac{0}{0} \) indeterminate form.

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   - Differentiate the numerator: \( \frac{d}{dx} \ln(1+x) = \frac{1}{1+x} \).
   - Differentiate the denominator: \( \frac{d}{dx} x = 1 \).

3. **Evaluate the New Limit:**
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{\frac{1}{1+x}}{1} = \lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{1}{1+x} = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The limit is \( 1 \).

```json
{"Final Answer": "1"}
```

To solve the limit as x approaches infinity of (1 + 1/x)^x, I recognize that this expression is similar to the definition of the number e.

I recall that as x approaches infinity, (1 + 1/x)^x approaches e. This is a well-known limit in calculus.

Therefore, applying this knowledge directly leads me to conclude that the limit is equal to e.
</think>

To find the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right)^x
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Recognize the Standard Limit:**
   The expression \(\left(1 + \frac{a}{n}\right)^n\) as \(n\) approaches infinity is a standard limit that equals \(e^a\), where \(e\) is Euler's number.

2. **Apply the Limit:**
   Comparing this with our given expression:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right)^x = e^{1} = e
   \]

**Final Answer:**

```json
{"Final Answer": "e"}
```

The limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of the function \( \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1} \) can be found by examining the leading terms in both the numerator and the denominator.

**Solution:**

We divide each term in the numerator and the denominator by \( x^2 \):

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{x^2(1 + \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2})}{x^2(1 - \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2})}
\]

This simplifies to:

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{1 + 0 + 0}{1 - 0 + 0} = 1
\]

\[

\]

\[

\]
where \( k \) is a constant.

   \]

   \[

   \]

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), I'll apply basic differentiation rules. 

First, I'll differentiate each term separately.

The derivative of \( 3x^2 \) is calculated using the power rule: bring down the exponent as a coefficient and reduce the exponent by one. This gives \( 6x \).

Next, the derivative of \( -5x \) is found by reducing the exponent from 1 to 0, which results in \( -5 \).

Finally, the constant term \( 4 \) differentiates to 0 because the slope of a constant function is zero.

Combining these results, the derivative \( f'(x) \) is \( 6x - 5 \).

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we'll apply basic differentiation rules to each term separately.

1. **Differentiate \( 3x^2 \):**
   Using the power rule, which states that the derivative of \( x^n \) is \( nx^{n-1} \),
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 3 \cdot 2x^{2-1} = 6x
   \]

2. **Differentiate \( -5x \):**
   The derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is 1, so,
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5 \cdot 1 = -5
   \]

3. **Differentiate the constant term \( 4 \):**
   The derivative of any constant is 0.
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
   \]

**Combining all the derivatives:**
\[
f'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
\]

**Final Answer:**  
The derivative of \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \) is:
```json
{"Final Answer": "6x - 5"}
```

The derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to \( x \) is calculated as follows:

First, rewrite \( \frac{1}{x} \) using a negative exponent:
\[ \frac{1}{x} = x^{-1} \]

Apply the power rule for differentiation, which states that the derivative of \( x^n \) is \( n \cdot x^{n-1} \):
\[ \frac{d}{dx}(x^{-1}) = -1 \cdot x^{-2} \]

Rewrite the expression back in fractional form:
\[ -1 \cdot x^{-2} = -\frac{1}{x^2} \]

I remember from logarithm properties that ln(a) - ln(b) = ln(a/b). Applying that here, this expression simplifies to:

= limₕ→₀ [ln(1 + (h/x)) / (h/x)] * (1/x).

Therefore, the entire expression simplifies to:

1 * (1/x) = 1/x.

So putting it all together, the derivative f'(x) is indeed 1/x. That makes sense and matches what I remembered from calculus.

Alright! Let's solve the problem step by step.

**Problem:**  
Given the function:
\[ F(x) = x^4 - 3x^3 + x \]

We need to find its first and second derivatives.

---

### Step 1: Find the First Derivative \( F'(x) \)

To find the first derivative, we'll apply the **Power Rule**, which states that if \( f(x) = x^n \), then \( f'(x) = n \cdot x^{n-1} \).

Let's differentiate each term of \( F(x) \):

1. **Differentiate \( x^4 \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(x^4) = 4x^{4-1} = 4x^3
   \]

2. **Differentiate \( -3x^3 \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(-3x^3) = -3 \cdot 3x^{3-1} = -9x^2
   \]

3. **Differentiate \( x \) (which is the same as \( x^1 \)):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1x^{1-1} = 1
   \]

Now, combine these results:
\[
F'(x) = 4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1
\]

---

### Step 2: Find the Second Derivative \( F''(x) \)

To find the second derivative, we'll differentiate \( F'(x) \).

Differentiate each term of \( F'(x) = 4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1 \):

1. **Differentiate \( 4x^3 \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(4x^3) = 4 \cdot 3x^{3-1} = 12x^2
   \]

2. **Differentiate \( -9x^2 \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(-9x^2) = -9 \cdot 2x^{2-1} = -18x
   \]

3. **Differentiate the constant term \( 1 \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(1) = 0
   \]

Now, combine these results:
\[
F''(x) = 12x^2 - 18x
\]

---

### Final Answer

The first and second derivatives of the function are:

\[
\boxed{
\begin{aligned}
F'(x) &= 4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1 \\
F''(x) &= 12x^2 - 18x
\end{aligned}
}
\]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \cos(x) \cdot \ln(x) \), we will use the **Product Rule**. The product rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), then its derivative is:

\[
h'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

In this case:
- Let \( u(x) = \cos(x) \)
- Let \( v(x) = \ln(x) \)

First, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
1. \( u'(x) = -\sin(x) \) (since the derivative of \( \cos(x) \) is \( -\sin(x) \))
2. \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \) (since the derivative of \( \ln(x) \) is \( \frac{1}{x} \))

Now, apply the product rule:

\[
f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]
\[
f'(x) = (-\sin(x)) \cdot \ln(x) + \cos(x) \cdot \frac{1}{x}
\]

Simplify the expression:

\[
f'(x) = -\sin(x)\ln(x) + \frac{\cos(x)}{x}
\]

**Final Answer:**

To find the derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \), we can use logarithmic differentiation.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Let \( y = x^x \).**
2. **Take the natural logarithm of both sides:**
   \[
   \ln(y) = \ln(x^x)
   \]
3. **Simplify using logarithm properties:**
   \[
   \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x)
   \]
4. **Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):**
   - Left side:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}[\ln(y)] = \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx}
     \]
   - Right side (using the product rule):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}[x \cdot \ln(x)] = \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \ln(x) + 1
     \]
5. **Set the derivatives equal to each other:**
   \[
   \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \ln(x) + 1
   \]
6. **Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):**
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = y \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]
7. **Substitute back \( y = x^x \):**
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = x^x \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{x^{x}(\ln(x)+1)}

To find the second derivative of \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \), we'll proceed step by step.

**Step 1: Find the First Derivative**

We start by applying the product rule to differentiate \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \). The product rule states that if you have a function \( u(x)v(x) \), its derivative is \( u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) \).

Let:
- \( u(x) = x^2 \)
- \( v(x) = e^x \)

\]
\[
v'(x) = e^x
\]

Now, apply the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
\]

**Step 2: Find the Second Derivative**

Next, we differentiate \( F'(x) \) to find the second derivative.

F'(x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x

   - Let \( v_1(x) = e^x \)

   \]
   \[
   v_1'(x) = e^x
   \]

   \]

Alright, so I have this problem where I need to find the derivative of the function f(x) = 1/(x² + 1). Hmm, okay. I've heard that derivatives are about finding the rate at which something changes, right? So in calculus terms, it's about how f(x) changes as x changes.

First, let me recall some basic rules for differentiation. There's the power rule, product rule, quotient rule... Since this function is a fraction, maybe I should use the quotient rule here. The quotient rule says that if you have a function like g(x)/h(x), its derivative is (g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x))/[h(x)]².

So in my case, f(x) = 1/(x² + 1). Let me rewrite this as f(x) = numerator/denominator where numerator is 1 and denominator is x² + 1. Applying the quotient rule, I'll need to find the derivatives of both the numerator and the denominator.

The derivative of the numerator (which is just 1) should be zero because the slope of a constant function is zero everywhere. So g'(x) = 0.

Now for the denominator h(x) = x² + 1. The derivative of that, h'(x), would be 2x, since the derivative of x² is 2x and the derivative of 1 (a constant) is zero. So h'(x) = 2x.

Putting it all into the quotient rule formula: f'(x) = [g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)]/[h(x)]² = [0*(x² + 1) - 1*(2x)]/(x² + 1)². Simplifying that, the numerator becomes -2x and the denominator remains (x² + 1) squared.

So using the power rule and the chain rule, I bring down the exponent, subtract one from it (so -1 becomes -2), multiply by the derivative of the inside function x² +1, which is 2x. So that would give f'(x) = -1*[x² +1]^{-2} * 2x = -2x/(x² +1)^2.

Wait, both methods gave me the same answer! That's reassuring. I think that confirms that whether I use the quotient rule or rewrite it as a power and apply the chain rule, I end up with f'(x) = -2x/(x² + 1)².

I should double-check my steps to make sure I didn't make any mistakes. Let me go through each part again:

- Identified that the function is a quotient: yes.
- Applied quotient rule correctly: numerator derivative times denominator minus numerator times denominator derivative, all over denominator squared. Yes.
- Calculated derivatives of 1 and x² +1: both correct (0 and 2x respectively).
- Simplified the expression correctly to get -2x/(x² +1)^2.

Alternatively with chain rule:

- Rewrote f(x) as [x² +1]^{-1}.
- Applied power rule: bring down the exponent, subtract one (-1 becomes -2), multiplied by derivative of inside function (which is 2x).
- Multiplied all together correctly to get -2x/(x²+1)^2.

Seems solid. Is there any other way to verify this? Maybe plugging in a value and checking numerically?

\]

To find the derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) with respect to \( x \), we'll use the chain rule.

**Solution:**

1. **Identify the outer and inner functions:**
   - Outer function (\( f(u) \)) = \( e^u \)
   - Inner function (\( u(x) \)) = \( x^2 \)

2. **Apply the chain rule:**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} [e^{u(x)}] = e^{u(x)} \cdot \frac{du}{dx}
   \]

3. **Differentiate the inner function with respect to \( x \):**
   \[
   \frac{du}{dx} = \frac{d}{dx}(x^2) = 2x
   \]

4. **Substitute back into the chain rule formula:**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} [e^{x^2}] = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{2 x e^{x^{2}}}

The function f(x) = \frac{\ln x}{x^2} can be differentiated using the quotient rule. Here's how:

**Step 1: Identify u and v**
- Let \( u = \ln x \)
- Let \( v = x^2 \)

**Step 2: Find derivatives of u and v**
- \( u' = \frac{d}{dx} (\ln x) = \frac{1}{x} \)
- \( v' = \frac{d}{dx} (x^2) = 2x \)

**Step 3: Apply the quotient rule**
The quotient rule states that:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2}
\]
Substituting u, v, u', and v':
\[
f'(x) = \frac{\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)(x^2) - (\ln x)(2x)}{(x^2)^2} = \frac{x - 2x \ln x}{x^4}
\]

**Step 4: Simplify the expression**
Factor out x in the numerator:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{x(1 - 2 \ln x)}{x^4} = \frac{1 - 2 \ln x}{x^3}
\]

The final answer is:

```json
{"Final Answer": "(1 - 2 ln(x)) / x³"}
```

The derivative of \( f(x) = \ln(\sin x) \) can be found using the chain rule.

1. **Identify the outer and inner functions**:
   - Outer function: \( \ln(u) \)
   - Inner function: \( u = \sin x \)

2. **Differentiate the outer function with respect to \( u \)**:
   \[
   \frac{d}{du} [\ln(u)] = \frac{1}{u}
   \]

3. **Differentiate the inner function with respect to \( x \)**:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} [\sin x] = \cos x
   \]

4. **Apply the chain rule** by multiplying the derivatives:
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{1}{u} \cdot \cos x = \frac{\cos x}{\sin x}
   \]

5. **Simplify the expression**, recognizing that \( \frac{\cos x}{\sin x} \) is the definition of cotangent:
   \[
   f'(x) = \cot x
   \]

The second derivative of \( F(x) = x^3 \ln x \) is found by first applying the product rule to find the first derivative and then differentiating again using the product rule on one term.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **First Derivative (F'(x)):**
   - Let \( u(x) = x^3 \) and \( v(x) = \ln x \).
   - Compute \( u'(x) = 3x^2 \) and \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \).
   - Apply the product rule: 
     \[
     F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) = 3x^2 \ln x + x^3 \cdot \frac{1}{x} = 3x^2 \ln x + x^2
     \]

2. **Second Derivative (F''(x)):**
   - Differentiate \( F'(x) \):
     - For the term \( 3x^2 \ln x \), use the product rule again.
       - Let \( u_1 = 3x^2 \) and \( v_1 = \ln x \).
       - Compute \( u_1' = 6x \) and \( v_1' = \frac{1}{x} \).
       - Apply the product rule:
         \[
         \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2 \ln x) = 6x \ln x + 3x
         \]
     - For the term \( x^2 \), compute the derivative: \( 2x \).
   - Combine both results:
     \[
     F''(x) = 6x \ln x + 3x + 2x = 6x \ln x + 5x
     \]
   
**Final Answer:**  
{'Final Answer': '6x ln(x) + 5x'}

The function f(x) = \(\frac{e^x}{1 + e^x}\). To find its derivative, we use the quotient rule.

Applying the quotient rule:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{(e^x)'(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x)'}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]
Simplifying the derivatives and terms:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{e^x (1 + e^x) - e^{2x}}{(1 + e^x)^2} = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

So, the derivative is \( f'(x) = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2} \).

To find the second derivative of the function \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \), we'll proceed step by step.

**First Derivative:**

Using the chain rule, the first derivative of \( F(x) \) is:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} \left( e^{x^2} \right) = 2x \cdot e^{x^2}
\]

**Second Derivative:**

Now, we differentiate \( F'(x) \) to find the second derivative. We'll use the product rule for differentiation:
\[
F''(x) = \frac{d}{dx} \left( 2x \cdot e^{x^2} \right)
\]
Applying the product rule (\( u'v + uv' \)) where \( u = 2x \) and \( v = e^{x^2} \):
\[
F''(x) = 2 \cdot e^{x^2} + 2x \cdot \frac{d}{dx}\left( e^{x^2} \right)
\]
We already know that \( \frac{d}{dx}\left( e^{x^2} \right) = 2x \cdot e^{x^2} \), so:
\[
F''(x) = 2e^{x^2} + 2x \cdot (2x \cdot e^{x^2}) = 2e^{x^2} + 4x^2 e^{x^2}
\]
Combining the terms, we get:
\[
F''(x) = (2 + 4x^2) e^{x^2}
\]

**Final Answer:**

The second derivative of \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \) is:
\[
\boxed{(2 + 4x^2)e^{x^2}}

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is given by:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{v(x) \cdot u'(x) - u(x) \cdot v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

**Step 1: Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

- Let \( u(x) = x \)
- Let \( v(x) = 1 + x^2 \)

**Step 2: Compute the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

- The derivative of \( u(x) = x \) is:
\[
u'(x) = 1
\]
  
- The derivative of \( v(x) = 1 + x^2 \) is:
\[
v'(x) = 2x
\]

**Step 3: Apply the Quotient Rule**

Plugging these into the quotient rule formula:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{(1 + x^2)(1) - x(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

Simplify the numerator:

\[
(1 + x^2) - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2
\]

So, the derivative becomes:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of sec(x) with respect to x can be found using the reciprocal rule for derivatives. Since sec(x) = 1/cos(x), applying the rule gives us:

d/dx [sec(x)] = (-sin(x)) / cos²(x)

This simplifies to:

sec(x)tan(x)

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Express sec(x):**
   - sec(x) is the reciprocal of cos(x): sec(x) = 1/cos(x).

2. **Apply the Reciprocal Rule:**
   - If f(x) = 1/g(x), then f'(x) = -g'(x)/[g(x)]².
   
3. **Differentiate cos(x):**
   - The derivative of cos(x) is -sin(x): d/dx [cos(x)] = -sin(x).

4. **Substitute into the Reciprocal Rule:**
   - f'(x) = -(-sin(x)) / [cos(x)]² = sin(x)/cos²(x).

5. **Express in Terms of Trigonometric Functions:**
   - Recognize that 1/cos(x) is sec(x), and sin(x)/cos(x) is tan(x).
   - Therefore, f'(x) = sec(x)tan(x).

**Final Answer:**

```json
{
  "Final Answer": "sec(x) * tan(x)"
}
```

The given problem is to find the limit of the function as \( x \) approaches infinity:

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{{5x^3 + 2x}}{{7x^3 + 3x^2}}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the highest degree terms in numerator and denominator:**
   - Numerator: \(5x^3\) (highest degree)
   - Denominator: \(7x^3\) (highest degree)

2. **Divide both the numerator and the denominator by the highest power of \( x \) present, which is \( x^3 \):**

\[
\frac{{5x^3 + 2x}}{{7x^3 + 3x^2}} = \frac{{5 + \frac{2}{x^2}}}{{7 + \frac{3}{x}}}

To find the limit:
\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\ln(1 + x)}{x}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Indeterminate Form:**
   
   As \( x \) approaches 0, both the numerator \( \ln(1+x) \) and the denominator \( x \) approach 0:
   \[
   \ln(1 + 0) = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad 0
   \]
   This results in the indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \).

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   
   L'Hospital's Rule states that if \( \lim_{x \to c} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} \) results in an indeterminate form of type \( \frac{0}{0} \) or \( \frac{\infty}{\infty} \), then:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to c} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{x \to c} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}
   \]
   provided the limit on the right exists.

3. **Differentiate the Numerator and Denominator:**
   
   - The derivative of \( f(x) = \ln(1 + x) \) is:
     \[
     f'(x) = \frac{1}{1 + x}
     \]
     
   - The derivative of \( g(x) = x \) is:
     \[
     g'(x) = 1
     \]

4. **Compute the New Limit:**
   
   Applying L'Hospital's Rule:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\ln(1 + x)}{x} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\frac{1}{1 + x}}{1} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1}{1 + x}
   \]

5. **Evaluate the Limit:**
   
   Substitute \( x = 0 \) into \( \frac{1}{1 + x} \):
   \[
   \frac{1}{1 + 0} = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{1}
\]

The derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is found using fundamental principles from calculus.

**Solution:**

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = e^x \):

1. Recall the definition of a derivative:
   \[
   f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x + h) - f(x)}{h}
   \]

2. Apply this to \( f(x) = e^x \):
   \[
   f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^{x+h} - e^x}{h}
   \]

3. Factor out \( e^x \) from the numerator:
   \[
   f'(x) = e^x \cdot \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^h - 1}{h}
   \]

4. Use the known limit \( \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^h - 1}{h} = 1 \):
   \[
   f'(x) = e^x \cdot 1 = e^x
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
{'Final Answer': 'e^x'}

To find the derivative of \( \frac{d}{dx}(x^x) \), we use logarithmic differentiation:

1. Let \( y = x^x \).
2. Take the natural logarithm: \( \ln(y) = x \ln(x) \).
3. Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):
   - Left side: \( \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} \)
   - Right side: Using product rule, \( 1 \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \ln(x) + 1 \).
4. Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \): \( \frac{dy}{dx} = y (\ln(x) + 1) \).
5. Substitute back \( y = x^x \): \( \frac{dy}{dx} = x^x (\ln(x) + 1) \).

**Answer:**  
The derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( x^x (1 + \ln(x)) \).

To find the derivative of \( \frac{d}{dx} (e^{x^2}) \), we use the chain rule. The outer function is \( e^u \) where \( u = x^2 \). The derivative of \( e^u \) with respect to \( x \) is \( e^u \cdot \frac{du}{dx} \).

1. Compute \( \frac{du}{dx} \):
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(x^2) = 2x
   \]

2. Apply the chain rule:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (e^{x^2}) = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x = 2x e^{x^2}

The function \( F(x) = x^3 \ln x \) is differentiated twice using the product rule and power rule. 

1. **First Derivative:**
   - Let \( u = x^3 \) ⇒ \( u' = 3x^2 \)
   - Let \( v = \ln x \) ⇒ \( v' = \frac{1}{x} \)
   - Applying the product rule: 
     \[
     F'(x) = 3x^2 \ln x + x^3 \cdot \frac{1}{x} = 3x^2 \ln x + x^2
     \]

2. **Second Derivative:**
   - Differentiate \( F'(x) = 3x^2 \ln x + x^2 \):
     - For \( 3x^2 \ln x \), apply the product rule again:
       - Let \( u_1 = 3x^2 \) ⇒ \( u_1' = 6x \)
       - Let \( v_1 = \ln x \) ⇒ \( v_1' = \frac{1}{x} \)
       - Result: \( 6x \ln x + 3x \)
     - For \( x^2 \), derivative is \( 2x \)
   - Combine the results:
     \[
     F''(x) = 6x \ln x + 3x + 2x = 6x \ln x + 5x
     \]

**Final Answer:**  
The second derivative of \( F(x) = x^3 \ln x \) is \( F''(x) = 6x \ln x + 5x \).

- Numerator: \(e^x\), Denominator: \(1 + e^x\)
- Derivative of numerator: \(e^x\)
- Derivative of denominator: \(e^x\)

To find the derivative of \( \tan^{-1} x \) with respect to \( x \):

Let \( y = \tan^{-1} x \).

Then,
\[
\tan y = x
\]

Differentiating both sides with respect to \( x \):
\[
\sec^2 y \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1
\]

Since \( \sec^2 y = 1 + \tan^2 y \) and \( \tan y = x \), we have:
\[
\sec^2 y = 1 + x^2
\]

Substituting back:
\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}
\]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = x \ln x \), we'll use the product rule. 

The product rule states:
\[ h'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) \]

Let \( u(x) = x \) and \( v(x) = \ln x \).

1. Compute \( u'(x) \):
   \[ u'(x) = 1 \]
   
2. Compute \( v'(x) \):
   \[ v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \]

3. Apply the product rule:
   \[ f'(x) = (1)(\ln x) + (x)\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) \]
   
4. Simplify:
   \[ f'(x) = \ln x + 1 \]

The second derivative of \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \) can be found by applying the chain rule twice and then using the product rule. 

First, find the first derivative:
\[ F'(x) = 2x e^{x^2} \]

Next, apply the product rule to find the second derivative:
\[
F''(x) = (2)'e^{x^2} + 2x(e^{x^2})'
\]
The derivative of \( 2x \) is 2, and using the chain rule again for \( e^{x^2} \):
\[
F''(x) = 2e^{x^2} + 2x(2x e^{x^2})
\]
Simplify:
\[
F''(x) = 2e^{x^2}(1 + 2x^2)

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \), we use the quotient rule.

**Quotient Rule**: If \( f(x) = \frac{g(x)}{h(x)} \), then 
\[
f'(x) = \frac{g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2}
\]

Here, let:
- \( g(x) = x \) ⇒ \( g'(x) = 1 \)
- \( h(x) = 1 + x^2 \) ⇒ \( h'(x) = 2x \)

Applying the quotient rule:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{1 \cdot (1 + x^2) - x \cdot 2x}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]
Simplify the numerator:
\[
1 + x^2 - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2
\]

Thus, the derivative is:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

The derivative of u = cos(x) with respect to x is -sin(x). Substituting this back into the expression for f'(x), I get:

To find the derivative of \(\sec(x)\), we can use the chain rule. Here's a step-by-step solution:

**Step 1:** Recall that \(\sec(x) = \frac{1}{\cos(x)}\).

**Step 2:** Apply the chain rule to differentiate \(\frac{1}{\cos(x)}\). Let \(u = \cos(x)\), so we have:
\[
f(x) = \frac{1}{u}
\]

**Step 3:** Differentiate \(f(x)\) with respect to \(u\):
\[
\frac{df}{du} = -\frac{1}{u^2}
\]

**Step 4:** Differentiate \(u\) with respect to \(x\):
\[
\frac{du}{dx} = -\sin(x)
\]

**Step 5:** Multiply the derivatives using the chain rule:
\[
\frac{df}{dx} = \frac{df}{du} \cdot \frac{du}{dx} = -\frac{1}{u^2} \cdot (-\sin(x)) = \frac{\sin(x)}{\cos^2(x)}
\]

**Step 6:** Express the result in terms of trigonometric functions:
\[
\frac{d}{dx} (\sec(x)) = \sec(x) \tan(x)
\]

**Final Answer:**
```json
{"Final Answer": "The derivative of sec(x) is sec(x) tan(x)."}
```
